- **2026-09-22 — the application still owns derived data (author).** After
  calibration #16 (0.2.32, holistic 88, `data_modeling` 82), Charlie asked three
  questions: whether a cross-cutting change would make the system more elegant,
  how to lift the weakest categories, and whether to refine Snapback 4 in place
  or start again. He then asked for a research LLP and plans. The research is
  [LLP 3000.012](./3000.012-application-still-owns-derived-data.research.md).
  Its proposals sit under the LLPs that own their subjects:
  [LLP 3000.007.003](./3000.007.003-the-engine-writes-derived-data.rfc.md)
  proposes that the engine write derived data, and asks for a ruling to narrow
  LLP 3000.007 §21.7's exclusion of join views to immutable references and
  capped fan-out.
  [LLP 3000.009.000](./3000.009.000-the-server-half-opt-in.plan.md) plans to put
  the server half behind `use server`. All three are Drafts. Nothing is
  accepted, commissioned or built, and no existing ruling changes.
  The same day, [Astra at max effort](./reviews/3000.012-application-still-owns-derived-data.astra.md)
  reviewed all three.
  - The research and the plan were READY WITH CHANGES; the RFC was NOT READY.
  - Each document records its dispositions. The RFC's revision 2 needs a delta
    review before any implementation.
  - The review also argued that these moves belong in Snapback 4, not in a
    fenced Snapback 5. LLP 3000.012 §3 records that view, and the author has
    not ruled on it.

- **2026-09-23 — a row or a grant born and undone inside one commit logs
  nothing.** The exhaustive disclosure explorer
  (`crates/snapback4-sim/tests/exhaustive.rs`) found that review finding 4's
  fix held for content but not for ids. A membership inserted and deleted in
  one commit still sent its grantee a `scope` naming the group, and sent the
  group's members a `del` naming a row that existed at no commit boundary.
  The ticket was
  [`issues/closed/20260923-snapback4-in-commit-grant-names-ids.md`](../issues/closed/20260923-snapback4-in-commit-grant-names-ids.md).
  Charlie commissioned the fix, the ticket's option (a), and it is built.
  `account_row_change` tracks, for the commit, the rows it inserted (in
  every table) and the grants it newly made (where the partition or the
  group changed). Each records the events it already had in the commit
  before its birth. The rule:
  - **A born row logs only its image at the commit's end.** Every change
    after its birth withdraws what it logged since birth. It logs no `del`,
    and deleting it leaves nothing.
  - **A born grant stays born until it is undone.** That holds through
    renewals, and through a new lifetime that ends it at once. Undoing it
    withdraws its joins since birth and logs no scope. Born siblings of one
    key share the birth until the last is gone.
  - **Events from before a birth always stay.** A row deleted and re-created
    under its id keeps its first deletion.
  - **A renewal of a grant held before the commit is not a birth,** so a
    revocation after one is always logged.
  - **Neither is a replacement.** A new grant beside a row of its key that
    existed before the commit (an expired predecessor not yet swept, which
    the log still holds) owes that predecessor's revocation, and its undoing
    is logged. A sibling born in the same commit is no predecessor.

  The key probes are unmetered log bookkeeping on the log-bearing host.
  Meter parity is about logical charges, and the device builds no log.

  Astra reviewed the change blind over five rounds
  ([record](./reviews/3000-in-commit-grant-names-ids.astra.md)):
  - **Round 1: NOT READY, three findings,** including a High: re-creating a
    row erased its first deletion, which happens naturally for a maintained
    count.
  - **Rounds 2–4 each found one more.** Round 2's was a High present since
    the first version: undoing a replacement erased an unswept predecessor's
    revocation. Rounds 3 and 4 found Mediums about same-commit siblings.
  - **Round 5: READY.**

  Every finding is a test in `reviews.rs` that fails on the version before
  its fix. The explorer gained a `churn` write that catches round 1's High on
  its own, and it enforces the rule with no tolerance.
  - **A pinned behavior changed.** The W13g same-commit `rejoin` fixture and
    its JS twin had pinned the old `join, scope, join`. They now expect one
    join and no scope.
  - **One inherited Medium is fix-forward.** A grant inserted already
    expired still names its row and group. It is
    [`issues/closed/20260923-snapback4-already-expired-grant-names-ids.md`](../issues/closed/20260923-snapback4-already-expired-grant-names-ids.md).

- **2026-09-23 — logical expiry revokes only a grant once held live.** This
  is a fix-forward from Astra's review of the entry above, ticket
  [`issues/closed/20260923-snapback4-already-expired-grant-names-ids.md`](../issues/closed/20260923-snapback4-already-expired-grant-names-ids.md).
  A grant inserted with its lifetime already over used to reach its grantee
  in two ways. Its row was re-read as absent and sent as a `del`, and
  logical expiry appended a scope naming its group. Now:
  - **A row born already expired logs no put.**
  - **Some chains are never live.** Its joins and their lifetimes are
    compared with each join's commit time, and in such a chain every join's
    explicit lifetime had ended by that commit. It gets no logical-expiry
    scope. A legacy join without a lifetime counts as live.
  - **Past the watermark, the chain is walked back.** When the watermark has
    passed the join, the chain is walked back to its last scope, so a live
    predecessor keeps its revocation.

  Astra's review is recorded in
  [the review record](./reviews/3000-already-expired-grant-names-ids.astra.md).
  - Round 1 was NOT READY: the group leaked on the poll after the one that
    consumed the join.
  - Round 2 closed that and found no dropped revocation, and flagged a
    quadratic rare path, now fixed.
  - A physical sweep of such a row still logs its `del` and scope. That
    needs the write path to see the principal's log, a decision left open on
    the ticket.

- **2026-09-23 — Quarry states intent (LLP 3000.013).** Charlie set the goal
  that Snapback 4 be holistically excellent, elegant and better than Supabase
  and Convex, and score 95 or better on Clique's `data_modeling`,
  `background_work`, `scale_and_limits`, `queries_and_live` and
  `client_integration`. He also authorized significant architectural rework
  ("I'm not attached to things being the way they are now"). After calibration
  #18 left those categories at 87, 89, 90, 90 and 92, [LLP
  3000.013](./3000.013-quarry-states-intent.rfc.md) decides the rework:
  - a planner that chooses and synthesizes every index, so reads state
    predicates and order;
  - one derived-data declaration, `view`: planned, maintained or materialized
    by the engine;
  - Following without caps, through engine-owned background delivery;
  - streaming aggregates, self-chunking jobs and `emit … once`;
  - an app object and an offline web shell in the client.

  The engine, rules, partitions, prediction and meters stay. Old spellings
  keep compiling. Implementation runs in five Astra xhigh lanes on
  `expo-build-1000`, each blind-reviewed by two families.

- **2026-09-24 — Quarry states intent is implemented** ([LLP
  3000.013](./3000.013-quarry-states-intent.rfc.md) §11). All seven lanes are
  merged through four integration rounds, and each was reviewed blind by Astra
  and Grok through fix rounds. The integrated suite has 0 failures. Calibration
  #18's Clique backend, restated in the new forms, compiles and runs end to end.
  `snapback4@0.4.0` and `snapback4-expo@0.4.0` follow, then calibration #19 on
  the frozen instrument.
- **2026-09-24 — a row whose lifetime ended by its own commit's end is
  retired in it (author).** This finishes the already-expired grant ticket
  (`issues/closed/20260923-snapback4-already-expired-grant-names-ids.md`).
  Charlie chose retirement at commit over giving the write path log access
  or recording liveness.
  - **What runs.** At the end of every program commit, `Runner::
    retire_expired_births` removes each row born in the commit, in a table
    with an `expire` column, whose image is expired at the commit's `now`.
    That covers mutations, job chunks, native chunks and the Postgres
    adapter.
  - **How.** It uses the sweep's own `remove`: no rules, and aggregates and
    events apply. Keys are taken in sorted order, under an unbounded meter.
  - **Aggregates take one net change per row.** The changes are applied after
    every removal. Removing rows one at a time could reach a total no commit
    held: a sum at the limit, with −1 and +1 both born expired, overflows if
    the −1 goes first. Only a total the commit really ends with can overflow.
  - **Linear work.** Retirement removes only expired rows, so whether an
    audience key keeps a live row cannot change during it, and each key is
    scanned once. Without that, n expired siblings of one grant cost
    n(n−1)/2 row visits.
  - **A maintained table cannot expire.** Its rows are the engine's to
    write, and the sweep and retirement delete as source writes. The schema
    refuses `expire` on a maintained target.
  - **Why it logs nothing.** Those rows are born, so they log nothing, and a
    born grant logs no scope. A replacement beside a pre-commit predecessor
    is not a born grant, so its retirement logs the predecessor's owed
    scope.
  - **Inside the commit the row exists as written.** A row revived to a live
    lifetime before the commit ends survives.
  - **Metering.** The program's metering is recorded first. The device's
    prediction does not retire, and neither side charges retirement.
  - **Exemptions.** Seeds, loads, activation and the sweep keep such rows
    for the sweep. They are not application writes.

  Astra reviewed the change blind over two rounds
  ([record](./reviews/3000-retire-expired-births.astra.md)). Round 1 found
  no disclosure problem. It found three others, now fixed:
  - removal in id order could overflow a sum;
  - scans of expired siblings were quadratic;
  - a maintained table could be given `expire`.

  Round 2 was READY.

- **2026-09-25 — author ruling: restore bounded private walks** (B2 round 6;
  LLP 3000.015 §2). Round 5's claim that the author requested removal of the
  hidden-candidate ceiling was incorrect and is reversed. Every request's
  physical work remains bounded by declared ceilings. The prepaid allowance
  helper stays; private pages have a separate 8,192-candidate walk ceiling,
  constant per page. A caller's row behind at least that many unreadable
  siblings is unreachable by paging in that direction: an empty page has no
  cursor, identically in every world beyond the bound. Together with shared-cap
  unreadable occupancy, this is the sequence contract's accepted residual.
  The device's separate prediction-work budget remains in force.

- **2026-09-25 — 0.4.2 and the LLP 3000.015 baseline, one law.** `sb5/land`
  (B1 `written by`, B2 `sequence by`, B3 row-keyed views; LLP 3000.015)

**2026-09-25 — authorized ephemeral tables (LLP 3000.017).** The standing
LLP 3000.009 §12 realtime line is enacted as `ephemeral <life>` tables in
one server process. Their mutations use a durable read snapshot and a
rollback-guarded memory hub, independent epochs and interest-filtered polls;
every renewal rechecks the insert rule. No durable sequence, log, replay,
outbox or device custody is created. Durable schemas retain their captured
serialization and emitted TypeScript. Last-seen storage, multi-server
presence and ephemeral authority over durable invariants stay excluded.

- **2026-09-26 — 0.4.3: receipts in one query, presence that stays current**

    5-second refresh, and clients re-run it in the background, keeping the

    Four rounds of an event-driven follow design are recorded as deferred.

    certifies the paved Following feed as constant once background cleanup

### Explicit sync after call

**2026-09-28 — author ruling, lane SY.** `await client.sync()` waits for a
sync round begun after that call, so successful sync observes server commits
acknowledged before it; a response already in flight may predate those commits.
With no round in flight it starts one. Callers arriving during the same round
share one follow-up, even if the older round fails; callers arriving during
that follow-up require the next round. Wakes, timers, reconnects and change
notifications retain ordinary coalescing. The follow-up runs the existing
acquisition and publication path on browser, process, Expo and custom handles.
Its errors and offline result keep their existing meaning (network failures
resolve without a value and set the link offline); the older round's result
cannot fulfill the explicit request. Close, sign-out and persistent restore
refusals retain their lifecycle fences.

### Contended read serving

**2026-10-02 — CT round 0.** RequestAdmission remains the owner of admission:
32 short, 32 mutation, 32 sync, 32 wait, 16 route, four registry and four
verifier requests. Its classification, permits, refusal bytes, counters,
nonblocking write and no-body-drain path are unchanged. Each class now owns a
lazy persistent worker pool, bounded by that same class limit. Both queued
and executing work own the original permit; there is no second admission
budget. Queue depth plus reserved idle workers determines whether another
worker is needed, so a notified but not yet scheduled worker cannot hide
needed capacity. Spawn failure drops the request guard on the dispatcher and
returns the existing refusal. Unwind builds catch each request panic and reuse
the worker; the production abort policy is unchanged. Idle workers terminate
when their admission owner drops; they retain no engine or request.

**2026-10-02 — CT round 1 correction.** Darwin request workers **and the
entire intake path** (serve loop, accept and connection parsing) use
`QOS_CLASS_USER_INITIATED`. The listener creator establishes that class before
creating any intake threads. A failed setter is counted and logged by thread class (HTTP startup, HTTP,
maintenance or background), with the last errno and Unix-millisecond failure
time for each class. `doctor` on a live dev host exposes those records as a
scheduling problem. Startup separately records its QoS readback and refuses
before opening the listener unless it establishes default-or-higher inheritance.
A later setter failure never establishes or replaces the startup readback, and
`doctor` makes no current HTTP inheritance claim from a failure count. Intake is never deliberately
demoted to utility. Linux's dispatcher and parsers retain inherited priority;
request creators are never niced down.

**2026-10-02 — CT round 4, operator ruling (supersedes round 3).** No
thread that takes the maintenance gate, engine or writer is deliberately demoted.
Both maintenance threads, the source watcher and the worker supervisor use
Darwin default QoS / Linux inherited nice. Request/intake priority is unchanged.
Only helpers without engine/writer custody and separate Node/watchdog processes
retain utility/nice-5 demotion. Failed Linux `setpriority` calls are logged and
counted separately as `niceFailures`, with per-class errno/time records in
`doctor`; the watchdog carries its failure to the owning server's counters.

**2026-10-03 — DR startup ordering.** The diagnostics writer keeps CT's
background scheduling policy. After the last startup banner, both `dev` and
`serve` drain queued stdout/stderr output before `serve_requests` dispatches
work. One FIFO barrier acknowledges attempted writes and flushes to both
destinations. Enqueueing the barrier and waiting for acknowledgement share a
three-second bound; on timeout or a disconnected writer, startup continues
with best-effort output. Healthy output therefore precedes the first response;
stalled or broken output cannot prevent serving. This startup wait has no engine custody. Request and engine threads retain
nonblocking try-send diagnostics and never drain output.

**2026-10-04 — NZ integration, release completion.** `deploy` and `rollback`
use the same three-second bounded drain after their operation has returned and
all engine/deployment leases have dropped. Otherwise a short-lived command can
exit before the background writer emits its queued feed-adoption estimate.
Success and failure both attempt the drain; a stalled/broken writer does not
change the operation's exit status. This adds no drain to export publication,
witness commands or request/engine work. A child-process regression delays the
writer and checks output before process exit for both release outcomes.

The ordinary thread retains ephemeral expiry, physical reclamation, one atomic
schedule boundary per admission, action/effect lease recovery and deadlines,
and stale-asset sweeping. Cadences remain 100 ms ephemeral/age checks, one-second
ordinary ticks, fifth-tick leases/deadlines and sixtieth-tick assets. Claims and
settlements still enforce deadlines themselves. Bulk rotates one delivery,
one recount and one job slice. **Kind selection and clock-drain exclusion are
checked after engine admission.** A bulk eligibility precheck releases the gate
before admission and is checked again afterward. No maintenance gate spans a wait
for the engine or chunk execution. The engine serializes reading the bulk kind,
committing its slice and advancing the cursor; the brief gate protects only
coordination state. Thus an engine-free stalled bulk admission cannot prevent
an ordinary expiry slice from running. Actual exclusive engine ownership still
blocks other engine work until release; no scheduler can execute two writers
through that lock simultaneously.

The round-3 engine contention signal (queued/recent requests) selects a 250 µs
cooperative budget; quiet work gets 2 ms. The host also samples one-minute OS
load against available logical CPUs, cached for 100 ms, because detached reads
do not take engine tickets. That signal includes other tenants and lags bursts.
Recounts now check time after each complete source candidate and preserve the
cursor/total in the same transaction, including the expiring source's two ranges.
Delivery and Quarry jobs retain their row boundaries. Native jobs cannot split
inside their transaction/guest ABI: at most one invocation runs per slice, with
2,000,000,000 fuel, 1,000,000 host steps, 16,384 examined rows, 8,192 writes,
16 MiB heap and 1 MiB output. Native `Again` supplies the next durable boundary.
These are deterministic resource bounds, **not a wall-clock promise** for native
execution, one Quarry iteration, setup, storage commit or filesystem I/O.

Between contended useful slices the bulk worker sleeps with no shared lock for
seven times the consumed thread CPU (including atomic overruns), capped at 100 ms.
LU round 2 also charges empty kinds but combines their repayment with the next
useful slice, or repays it before an entirely empty cycle waits for work. Each
kind's charge is capped separately at 100 ms. Accumulated debt reaching 100 ms
triggers repayment even on an empty turn; each turn schedules at most 100 ms
of that debt and carries any remainder forward. No charge is lost by combining
empty turns.
Empty-cycle debt and slice-completion charges each append after any outstanding
repayment, whichever worker finishes first. LU round 3 fixes completion after
an empty-cycle flush: neither completion nor flushing can overlap or discard
the other's charge. Every sleep remains at most 100 ms.
An ordinary cheap empty recount/job therefore adds no separate sleep/wakeup to
every delivery slice. Request commits cannot cancel repayment.
Each kind still releases engine custody and re-enters FIFO admission separately.
There is no minimum pause that would overcharge cheap or empty turns. The CPU
clock excludes time already spent descheduled; a failed sample conservatively
falls back to wall time. Chunk deadlines still use wall time. This targets
one-eighth CPU duty for ordinary short slices; the cap and indivisible work
preclude a universal duty ceiling. The pause also applies if engine waiters
arrived during a quiet slice. Request commits do not cancel it. Quiet work has
no new charge, but preserves outstanding repayment; an entirely empty
three-kind cycle waits up to 100 ms for work.
After one second without a completed bulk turn, ordinary maintenance runs the
next kind itself, checking every 100 ms and after each ordinary engine slice.
Nominal minimum eligibility is **one turn per 1 s plus up to 100 ms polling,
every kind within three turns**, plus OS scheduling, already admitted FIFO work,
atomic row/commit/I/O delays or a synchronous clock drain. This reserves service
opportunities rather than a hard completion deadline. A finite healthy backlog
drains; parked/failing work needs repair and unbounded arrivals still have no
per-item wall-clock bound. The LU amendment below adds a delivery-turn bound.
No priority restoration is needed.

Delivery still commits before recount within each cycle. Recounts use their
durable basis/delta rules and need not wait for the entire delivery queue.
Clock callers serialize with each other through a separate lock; under the
engine they mark a drain and extend leases atomically with the clock change.
Periodic engine slices check that mark after admission and defer while the
request performs its original synchronous full-drain order. An unwind guard
clears the mark even after a request panic; durable cursor/restart and shutdown
semantics remain. The coordination gate never spans that drain. No durability,
admission, request-pool, read-custody or resource ceiling changes.

**2026-10-04 — LU round 1, live delivery with a bounded share.** The pacing
and engine admission above remain. The round-0 forward-key selector could
starve an old item forever if fresh work arrived before every turn; this
supersedes its progress claim. Background delivery now serves the queue head
and atomically appends unfinished work behind all currently pending items.
New work also appends at the tail. The existing engine-owned `Work.at` field
is a queue position, no longer an enqueue timestamp; source event timestamps,
admission tokens and edge epochs retain their meanings. A metered descending
one-row peek at the existing `byStatusTime` index supplies the next position.
Legacy timestamp positions remain valid, and equal or backwards clocks cannot
put a new item ahead of existing work. There is no new table, index or ceiling.

**Turn bound:** an item pending at time t in a declaration with P pending items
gets a slice within P successful visits to that declaration. Every predecessor
leaves or moves behind it, and arrivals also join behind it. With a fixed set
of D declarations and the existing declaration rotation, this is at most D×P
successful background delivery turns. P is frozen at t, not increased by later
arrivals; after service a new obligation uses the then-current P. Refusal
parking or cancellation ends that item's obligation. This guarantees service,
not finite completion under continually growing input or any wall-clock limit.
CT's three bulk kinds, admission, pacing, OS scheduling and atomic commits still
affect elapsed time. Repeated failed commits or resetting declaration rotation
through repeated open/adopt are outside the cross-declaration turn bound.

Queue positions persist with each slice's cursor, copies and epochs in one
transaction, so reopen/adopt preserves the per-declaration share. Only the next
declaration remains an in-memory hint. Explicit synchronous clock drains finish
the current queue head without requeuing it. Queue positions use the existing
exact-integer range; exhaustion refuses atomically rather than wrapping ahead.

**2026-10-04 — LU round 2, wide live fan-out.** A delivery walk starts with
two candidates (one useful row plus lookahead) and doubles its page only when
the current turn still has budget, up to the existing 32-row scan and 256-row
chunk limits. Short contended turns no longer repeatedly read a 32-row page
and discard 31 candidates. All candidates retain their ordinary row/rule/meter
checks. The cursor advances only through consumed rows and commits with their
copies and queue position. The P-visit bound above, 250 µs/2 ms cooperative
deadlines, CPU charge, aged opportunities and request admission remain intact.
Empty-kind repayment is combined as specified above; a wholly empty cycle
pays its debt before the interruptible idle wait.

The 900-follower replay fixture follows an author with no history, so it has
no queued backfills or cleanups; the only pending item is the live fan-out.
Round 2 records pre-LU, current-main and FIFO-ablation evidence rather than
attributing this fixture's delay to rotation behind nonexistent work. The Linux
measurements do not establish the cause of the reported Darwin timeout. A
finite fan-out still needs proportional copies: this policy improves the
measured last-recipient latency, not a width-independent wall-clock promise
for unbounded fan-out or an arbitrarily delayed host.

Arrival is **not FIFO by event time**: a later event can reach a follower before
an earlier multi-slice event. Each query page remains sorted by its declared
event order, with duplicate-free event identity and the existing
delivered-contents completeness contract. Client pages reconcile ordered query
snapshots; arrival order cannot be used as an event watermark or a notification
count. The [LU report](../snapback4/reports/20261003-lu-live-update-latency/REPORT.md)
records the client-surface audit, continuing-arrival regressions and CPU-confined
web measurements.

Round 2's ordinary-all policy had read p95 0.506 versus 0.415 ms demoted at 48 busy
processes and 3.425 versus 2.163 ms at 96; it improved at 144. Every corrected
mixed cell passed. The operator chose the split because the observed recovery
cost of demotion was small and phase dominated, while bulk delivery competes
with calibration probes. The read-pool idle-cost trade-off and quiet-Mac timing
remain unqualified. The delivery throughput problem is tracked separately in
[the product issue](../issues/20261002-snapback4-following-replay-delivery-backlog.md);
this scheduling change does not fix the delivery algorithm or revise Following
completeness. Round-3 measurements and limitations live in the CT report.

Round 3's corrected small fixture passes all twelve design/load cells, with
2,424,961 reads and all 72 abandoned claims observed. At 96 busy processes,
read p95 is 1.151 ms demoted-all, 2.655 ms ordinary-all and 1.232 ms split;
split is 53.6% below ordinary and 7.1% above demoted in that cell. At 0/48/144,
split p95 is 0.726/0.471/4.188 ms, within about 2.3% of each control. These sequential
shared-host observations do not establish a universal performance improvement
or a quiet-Mac result. The progress regression completes a queued job through
aged ordinary turns without starting a lower-priority thread.

The real 50%-scale replay qualification restores 6,507 pending deliveries for
each of twelve trials. At 96 busy processes, split's pooled read p95 is lower
than both controls on all eight builder queries; Following-500 is 9.738 ms
versus 12.897 ordinary-all and 11.132 demoted-all. Split drains in 124.531 and
167.005 s, between ordinary's 87.176/100.813 s and demoted's 260.588/185.044 s.
At zero owned busy processes, split drains in 32.577/37.699 s and loses to
ordinary p95 on five of eight queries. All settled hashes agree. The split-only
combined fixture also passes at every load, draining 1,000 fan-out tasks in
52.438/80.313/277.007/428.446 s at 0/48/96/144. These retain the bulk throughput
cost and the product backlog finding; there is no midpoint-pass claim.

Round 4 replaces the demoted lock holder with paced ordinary work. All twelve
mixed cells pass (2,117,919 reads, 72/72 abandoned claims, no unexpected lease
changes); the paced 1,000-task combined fixture drains in
143.037/220.737/693.048/1,177.746 s at 0/48/96/144 busy. Its read p95 during drain
is 0.751/1.348/3.041/5.318 ms during the 125-second read window. The small-fixture
comparison does not establish a consistent win: paced p95 at 144 is 3.633 ms
versus split 0.917 ms. Ordinary timing obligations still satisfy the fixture.

Sixteen fresh real-backlog trials restore the same 6,507-task checkpoint and
agree with round 3 on all eight settled answers. At 96 busy, paced pooled p95
beats ordinary-all on all eight queries and split on three; five are 0.8–3.8%
slower than split. Following-500 is 8.865 ms paced versus 9.609 split and
10.473 ordinary. Paced drains in 819.789/551.717 s versus split's
140.144/147.438 s: a substantial 3.7–5.9× throughput cost. At zero owned busy,
paced's two drain times are 712.475/32.443 s as shared-host load changes; lower
pooled p95 on all eight queries is not a quiet-host guarantee. Longer drains
contribute more probe cohorts and intermediate feed states. The
[round-4 record](../snapback4/reports/20261001-ct-contended-reads/REPORT.md#round-4--ordinary-lock-holders-and-paced-bulk-2026-10-02)
retains ranks, per-trial times, shared-host observations, the rejected wall-time
pacing candidate and final-source qualification. The lock-inversion regression
now starts the actual bulk thread and stalls it inside engine admission; a real
ordinary expiry completes with bulk still stopped and the engine free. Stopping
an exclusive engine owner still prevents concurrent engine execution.

Output-only helper threads use utility on Darwin and
lower their own Linux nice value to at least 5, without privileges. The supervised Node exec now uses Darwin
`posix_spawnattr_set_qos_class_np` with a utility task clamp, explicit stdin/
stdout/stderr actions and the existing separate child process group. Setting
only the watchdog pthread (round 0) did **not** establish the executed child's
class. Linux retains ordinary spawn and its inherited nice value. The owner
flock remains watchdog-only and closes on child exec. The operator's readback
loads an N-API probe inside the executed Node and checks effective Mach base
priorities across its main, V8, libuv and JavaScript worker threads, with an
unclamped control. The oracle requires utility priority 20 specifically; a
background clamp fails too. The operator ran the round-1 readbacks on an M5 Pro
Mac: all four thread tests and the executed-Node probe passed (13 Node threads
at 20, unclamped at 31). Round-2 changes still require the operator's Mac run;
cross-compilation is not runtime qualification.

Development clock advancement deliberately keeps the request's user-initiated
class while running its synchronous maintenance/jobs: the developer is waiting
for their completion. Ordinary effect leases remain fixed at claim, with no
heartbeat; the explicit advance extends held leases under LLP 3000.007 §20.12.

A connection-parser inline alternative was measured with the same 32-short
permit, panic boundary, body/write deadlines and refusal path. The 128-connection
cap and one outstanding request per connection remain unchanged. It did not
improve both idle and loaded results across the endpoints: whole-host untraced
idle following-500 is 4.335 ms pooled versus 5.070 ms inline; ten-CPU controls
reverse some comparisons but retain other regressions. Retain the pool and
preserve the alternative as a reproducible report patch. This is an explicit
idle/loaded trade-off for the operator, **not a no-idle-regression claim**.
CPU identities, kernel migrations and thread CPU time are retained; hardware
cycle access was denied on the qualification host. No affinity, governor,
utilization-clamp or real-time policy enters the product.

Every request-worker exit now decrements its live and idle accounting, including
unwind outside the request catch. **Round 2:** on any worker exit, the existing
pending-request guards refuse queued work and release permits off the queue
lock. Surviving workers may be blocked indefinitely; their existence cannot
strand an admitted request. A later submission can create a replacement within
the original class limit. Both partial-exit and last-exit queued paths are tested. Production's panic-abort policy remains unchanged.

File-backed query authentication and execution already use detached readers
(LLP 3000.019 §3.6); their fresh transactions, authority checks, reader epochs
and writer FIFO are unchanged. No answer cache is introduced. The Node token
client already reuses KA's private connection pool; its one proven transport
retry and non-idempotent exclusions are unchanged.

`scripts/contended-reads.mjs` replays calibration #28's actual fixture logs to
50%, verifies the builder's invariant manifest, and probes through the
published token client at 0, 0.5, 1 and 1.5 busy processes per available CPU.
It retains all 23 samples per probe, excludes the first three, and uses rank
19 of the remaining 20 for each cohort's p95. Five cohorts describe repeat
variability; their combined percentiles do not replace the original cohorts.
Before taking the first sample, the harness waits for durable Following delivery,
view recounts and jobs to drain, then requires an unchanged commit head for one
second. Parked work or a 30-minute wait fails qualification; no clock advance
manufactures settlement. The wait and queue samples are retained. A complete
Following answer alone does not establish quiescence: `guide feeds` permits
asynchronous new-event delivery without changing `complete`, with no hard
backlog deadline (LLP 3000.013 §4). Host telemetry has a 16 MiB process-output
cap and 2-second timeout; sampling failures are recorded and never kill a run.
An explicit dev-only `SNAPBACK4_READ_TRACE` file and numeric correlation header
record monotonic stage timestamps for qualification, without tokens, arguments,
answers or a remote inspection API. Normal serving retains no trace. The
bounded latency registry and its existing timing meanings are unchanged.

The structural regression observes at most 32 request thread identities across
1,000 sequential reads; the original dispatcher creates 1,000. Existing tests
retain every admission class, spawn-failure refusal, writer-independent read,
revocation, adoption and panic-recovery property. macOS readback tests are
cross-checked for aarch64-apple-darwin on Linux; the operator runs them on a Mac.
The harness also accepts `--mixed`: real-minute scheduled jobs, supervised
effect delivery and retries, deliberately abandoned fixed leases with worker
HTTP paused during requeue observation, deadline outcomes, physical expiry
reclamation and ordinary maintenance, alongside continuous reads. The deliberate
slow-effect probe is emitted after the lease-extension clock advance;
`--mixed --deadline-only` independently checks its real-clock outcome while
reads run, without advancing time. It does not
use replay or advance the clock during its observation window. Round 2 refuses
unexpected fixed-lease changes and requires every deliberately abandoned first
claim to appear in both worker events and polling observations, with matching
expiry. Maintenance-active read timings are retained for both ordinary and
demoted maintenance; the scheduling trade-off remains disclosed.
Measurements, limitations, source custody and every qualification failure:
[`CT report`](../snapback4/reports/20261001-ct-contended-reads/REPORT.md).


**2026-10-08 — S3 long-wait capacity (LLP 3000.036 §6 A).** The wait class is
split. Viewers' held `GET /changes?wait` and `POST /a/<action>` keep the wait
class, sized by `--long-waits` (default 1,024, at most 4,096); service workers'
`/effects/claim` and `/actions/claim` get their own claim class
(`--claim-waits`, default 32), so neither can starve the other. A change poll
without `wait` is writer-class work, not a long wait. RequestAdmission still
owns every permit; the per-principal share of held change polls
(`--long-waits-per-principal`, default 16) is taken inside the wait permit
after authentication and refused at once, never queued. The connection cap is
derived (128 + both pools). Thousands of waiters exposed two quadratic
herds, both fixed here: the engine's FIFO lock now parks each ticket on its own
condvar and wakes only the next one, and a parked change poll holds no reader
— polls woken by the same publication at the same cursor share one answer
read on a pooled reader (`serve_changes.rs` `answer`), while entry and unheld
polls still read the store; a pooled read that fails (an adoption committed
before the pool was replaced) is read again behind the engine. Measured costs are in
SERVER.md "Long-wait capacity".
### HTTP overload diagnostics

**2026-09-28 — Lane FU.** A zero-wait Clique replay can queue pending writes
across many clients faster than the host settles them. The 128-connection cap
and two-upload cap stay unchanged, including under `--fresh --replay`.
Connection saturation is an admission refusal: before allocating a parser or
worker, attempt one fixed, nonblocking HTTP 503 `E_RATE_LIMIT` with
`family: limit`, `retryable: true` and `Retry-After: 1`, then close. Full request
worker pools take the same path after header parsing, without allocating a
worker or draining the body. A request guard also refuses and counts a worker
thread-spawn failure before dropping the body reader on the dispatcher.
Existing leases are never evicted. The public
refusal contains no count, identity or application data; only this static
refusal allows `Access-Control-Allow-Origin: *` and exposes `Retry-After`.
Protected-response policy is unchanged. A refused preflight still fails CORS.
TLS transports without a usable response path close without cleartext HTTP;
the deployed binary continues to use HTTP behind its TLS proxy.

Every refusal increments saturating counters, including failed HTTP writes and
TLS closes. A successful write does not prove peer receipt. The serve loop
reports cumulative connection and per-worker-pool counts at most once per
second each; suppressed events remain counted. Reporting uses one process
writer and at most 32 fixed 4096-byte diagnostic lines. Producers use try-send;
output errors are ignored, never print panics. A stalled output can stall that
writer but cannot block acceptance or engine work, nor grow its queue. Counters
remain independent of best-effort output. No overload socket queue, parser,
body drain or rejection worker is introduced. The audit also moves request,
maintenance, adoption and supervised-worker diagnostics off engine paths.

The SDK validates refusal envelopes and preserves HTTP refusals before
transport failure handling, including sync. Overload does not mark the link
offline or end worker authentication; workers, including route verifiers,
delay and continue. Safe reads and downloads retry explicit retryable admission
refusals up to four attempts, with jitter and `Retry-After`, within ten seconds
of backoff. Jitter applies after taking the maximum with `Retry-After`, so
the advertised minimum does not synchronize early attempts. Uploads use the
same jitter with their existing four-attempt/ten-second policy;
same-ID mutations retain their outbox retry owner and add jitter and
`Retry-After`. A reset never automatically replays an upload or one-view download.
Malformed envelopes are status-bearing `E_HTTP_RESPONSE`; valid asset
missing/viewed refusals retain their meaning. Actual transport errors retain
available causes, with neutral wording when the host supplies none.

Schema discovery shares the bounded safe-read policy. Fresh local startup
throws the typed schema refusal (including exhausted `E_RATE_LIMIT`) rather
than substituting the device's never-synced `E_AUTH`; a saved backend may open
without marking a delivered refusal offline. Terminal authentication still
clears the session. Ephemeral calls preserve validated `denied` envelopes and
report malformed replies as status-bearing `E_HTTP_RESPONSE`, keeping the link
and waiting calls in order; each dispatched call is sent once, with no replay.
Change polling delays after retryable delivered refusals without marking the
link offline. Nonretryable refusals become visible denied reads and stop the
follower until the client is reopened.
A validated overload from a reconnect probe or sync also restores a link that
was offline; it proves peer reachability, not store identity or acquisition.
An unverified probe in either client tier waits with jitter after the refusal’s `Retry-After`
minimum before its recovery loop can try again, including nonretryable
`E_RATE_LIMIT`. Malformed probe replies also back off.

Credential rotation does not make a download idempotent. An ambiguous download
refusal survives rotation. AV round 3 corrects the successful-body case:
completed bytes stay with the same known principal without a second GET;
sign-out, close, store fences and an unknown principal still discard obsolete
handles. See LLP 3000.008.002.000 §6. Only proven non-consumption (a ranged request, authentication or retryable
admission refusal) permits another download with the new credentials. Local media fallback
returns retained ordinary bytes when available, otherwise the actual transport
cause. Auth endpoints preserve refusals/status/cause and are never immediately
replayed within one helper call. HTTP 200 without a valid session returns
status-bearing `E_HTTP_RESPONSE`, never a fabricated terminal auth failure.
Retryable `E_AUTH` is preserved without ending credentials.

**Supervisor ruling, 2026-09-28 (FU round 5):** refresh-token custody needs its
own design and lane. The round 3/4 instance fence, persisted claim, startup
suppression, warning and session-model I14 are removed. Dispatch, persistence,
startup and retry scheduling return to mainline behavior. This deliberately
retains the existing ambiguous-refresh limitation: a server can consume the
bearer and lose the reply, then a later refresh can resend it and sign out.
Only independent refusal decoding and retryable-auth handling remain here.
[The separate issue](../issues/20260928-snapback4-ambiguous-session-refresh.md)
records the cross-client, restart, non-dispatch, storage and replacement
counterexamples and requires its own LLP before implementation. Historical
round reports and append-only journal entries describe those revisions, not
a current custody guarantee.

**2026-10-02 — FDL rounds 0–2.** Before opening server resources, `dev` and
`serve` attempt once to raise soft `RLIMIT_NOFILE` to the hard limit, capped at
65,536. Darwin additionally caps the target at `OPEN_MAX` (10,240) and the
reported `kern.maxfilesperproc`. An already higher soft limit is preserved;
the hard limit and every admission cap remain unchanged. Failure never refuses
startup. Startup and live dev `doctor` report the server's effective soft/hard
limits, inherited soft limit, target and any OS cause. A live report replaces
doctor's own limits. Without one, `doctor` only observes its inherited limits
and explains the server's startup raise: soft 1,024 / hard 1,048,576 needs no
operator action. It warns only if the hard limit or platform ceiling keeps the
startup target below 2,048. A hard cap of 1,024 requires a shell/container/service
hard limit change; a lower platform ceiling requires a host ceiling change,
followed by server restart. A live effective limit below 2,048 warns using that
server's limits and directs an otherwise possible raise to its startup failure.
The warning threshold leaves headroom
above the measured parallel-server test demand; it is not a per-server minimum
or capacity guarantee. Children inherit the raised soft limit and unchanged
hard limit; Node raises its own further, up to the hard limit. The 65,536 cap
belongs to the server's adjustment, not the complete process tree.

Shared test initialization runs before each direct listener/server creation,
including constrained latency sockets, the HTTP integration binary and mock
listeners in replay, token and witness tests, and the latency/token UDP route
probes; it does not rely on test order.
Child dev/serve processes initialize themselves before their first server.
Tests that deliberately exhaust descriptors lower them only after initialization
in isolated children, preserving the parent runner's limits.

The parallel binary suite's descriptor demand is legitimate overlapping
servers, SQLite readers and connections: FDL measured 1,197 descriptors with
96 tests active, versus 249 in a serial run returning to three descriptors.
Descriptor stability is supported only over the observed workloads: Astra's
round-0 live probe served 38,400 keep-alive requests over 30 cycles, returning
from 268 to its 12-descriptor baseline, including idle expiry. No hours-long
stability claim is made. The operator's office M1 runtime evidence on
`f3870cd985` is three of three whole binary unit-suite runs passing 238/238 at
soft 256, with `ulimit -Hn` unlimited, launchd soft 256 and
`kern.maxfilesperproc` 245,760. This is round-0 runtime evidence, not a macOS
execution of round 1's new backoff test.

Each connection owns read/write, deadline and abort descriptors; requests
share the connection's abort handle instead of duplicating it again. Fallible
clones close the failed connection and release its admission lease. These are
transport closes, not admission refusals. EMFILE/ENFILE from accept or any of
the three clone stages increments a separate, process-wide saturating counter
reported by live `doctor`. Intake sleeps 1 ms, doubling to a 100 ms cap, after
releasing the failed connection; only complete connection construction resets
the delay. Accept exhaustion also retries, allowing recovery when descriptors
free; other accept errors remain `E_HTTP_LISTENER` with their OS cause. The
Linux real-backlog test measures accept-thread CPU with zero, one, two and
three spare descriptors, checks the counter and subsequent serving; removing
the sleep makes every case fail. Existing admission refusal accounting is
unchanged. Evidence:
[`FDL report`](../snapback4/reports/20261002-fdl-fd-limit/REPORT.md), including
round 2's doctor and UDP probe corrections.
Lost replies cannot be retrospectively
classified by the client. Connection saturation and worker saturation have
separate operator counters; round 0's resets did not prove which admission
layer refused each call. See the round 1 report for the corrected attribution.

The Linux reproduction hit the connection cap with at most 563 server file
descriptors. The launching shell has a 1024 soft limit; Node raises its own
limit to 1,048,576 before spawning the server. The server stayed alive and answered
after the failed upload. The driver with positive write wait passed later
checkpoints on the same published server. Evidence and final results:
[`Lane FU report`](../snapback4/reports/20260928-fu-fresh-replay-uploads/REPORT.md).


**2026-09-30 — Lane KA, connection reuse.** Bounded HTTP responses advertise
`Connection: keep-alive` and `Keep-Alive: timeout=5`; the timeout is the
whole-second floor of the smaller idle and header limits. HTTP/1.0 persists
only when requested. An exact `close` or `upgrade` token, across all Connection
fields, wins; terminal responses advertise close without an idle hint. The same
policy frames ordinary responses, coalesced JSON and heartbeat streams. Idle
and header deadlines begin between completed requests. A held `/changes` reply
keeps its independent 25-second hold and two-second heartbeats; the idle hint
is not a response deadline. Admission limits, refusals and TLS policy stay as
above. The deployed topology remains a TLS terminator in front of HTTP `serve`.

The Node SDK's private pinned undici agent caps idle retention at one second,
including when a peer advertises a longer timeout. Its transport retries once,
on a fresh non-pooling agent, only after ECONNRESET, EPIPE or UND_ERR_SOCKET on
a socket that completed an earlier SDK response, with zero newly received
bytes, no redirect and no abort. The ordinary agent uses HTTP/1.1 without
pipelined replay. Diagnostic events are correlated with the actual queued
request at Client dispatch, never another caller's async context. The retry
agent drains a streaming response before closing. URL, headers and materialized
body are captured at the original dispatch; edits to caller options cannot
change the retry's write ID, arguments or authority. Fresh failures, any received
response prefix (even an incomplete header), and a failed second attempt keep
the original error path to E_OFFLINE. Delivered E_RATE_LIMIT still belongs to
the existing admission-backoff policy.

Eligible requests are materialized POST query and sync bodies; GET schema,
probe, changes, write status, API/native artifact and root reads; and durable
mutations carrying their unchanged nonempty write ID and `retry_write: true`.
Durable SDK dispatchers, including exit delivery, add this envelope field.
The server checks old durable receipts first, then refuses the field on a
currently ephemeral operation before execution. This matters because an
ordinary ephemeral `/m/` call also has an ID but does not durably deduplicate,
and a hot adoption may change an operation's kind. Auth, actions, uploads,
asset GETs (which may consume one view), unmarked mutations, Request objects
and streamed request bodies receive no transport replay. The existing durable
outbox remains the owner of later recovery and settlement.

Chromium's socket pool runs outside renderer JavaScript and already recovers
reused-connection failures before response headers. React Native delegates to
NSURLSession on iOS and OkHttp on Android, outside the JS event loop too; a JS
pause cannot strand their socket-expiry callbacks as it can in Node. Their
native recovery is host-specific, not an SDK guarantee: NSURLSession can still
reject a POST after a connection loss, and React Native fetch does not reveal
reuse or response-byte progress. The default browser/Expo transport therefore
does not guess from opaque fetch failures or add a blanket replay. Actual
failures remain E_OFFLINE and durable writes retain outbox recovery. The new
server hints are available to all HTTP peers. Linux Chromium was exercised;
iOS/Android runtime behavior was source-reviewed, not simulator-qualified.

Calibration #27's ECONNRESET is consistent with this race, not retrospectively
proved by it. The retained baseline and fixed experiments, exact OS error
variants, counts and platform references are in
[`KA round 0`](../snapback4/reports/20260930-ka-keepalive-reuse/REPORT.md).


**2026-10-08 — S3.** A full viewer or claim pool no longer answers the
assets-guided `E_RATE_LIMIT`: the dispatcher writes the same single nonblocking
503 through `refuse_overload_with`, with `E_STREAMS` (family `limit`,
retryable, `guide wire`), `Retry-After: 2` and a body naming the pool and its
size. A principal past its share gets 429 `E_STREAMS` with `Retry-After: 5`
from the handler. Connection saturation and every other pool keep the fixed
`E_RATE_LIMIT` refusal; counters and the once-per-second report now list eight
pools.
### Publication suite on small hosts

The M5 MacBook is the publication host (author ruling 2026-10-02). The
office M1 is reserved for calibration; QF round 1's macOS checks use only the
Apartment mini. The historical M1 measurements below describe the earlier
2026-09-28 publication placement. The suite's client-file concurrency is based on Node's
`os.availableParallelism()`, which also respects Linux CPU affinity:
`max(1, min(24, floor(CPUs / 3)))`. One slot budgets a test file together
with its native server, device processes, workers and browser renderers.
Acquisition and server-e2e use the same pool, without additional shard pools.
An explicit `SNAPBACK4_TEST_SERIAL_CLIENT_FILES=1` selects one slot; the
default needs no publication override. Host-wide load averages do not
describe an affinity-restricted lane and no longer choose the pool size.
Rust compilation/tests precede clients; the full-count model files run in
their existing separate serial slot. Real-timer client fixtures listed in
`TIMER_CLIENT_FILES` in `scripts/run-client-tests.mjs` drain the ordinary pool
and run individually afterward; they keep their timing bounds. The quote-jump
browser witness remains in the ordinary pool. Scheduling changes no assertion, seed
count or timeout.

Publication may prewarm `cargo test --workspace --no-run` in its own
worktree with the same toolchain, default test profile, Cargo build inputs
and `LIBSQLITE3_FLAGS=-USQLITE_ENABLE_MEMORY_MANAGEMENT` as the dry run.
The suite still obtains its authoritative inventory from Cargo and executes
all test binaries plus doctests. A warm cache is preparation, never evidence
that the suite passed. See the [Lane MT report](../snapback4/reports/20260929-mt-ten-core-suite/REPORT.md)
and [M1 host ticket](../issues/20260928-snapback4-macos-qualification-host-office-m1.md)
for measurements and platform limits.

### Publication suite under contention

The suite runs on shared hosts. Test semantics must not depend on another
build yielding a CPU or on a child executable escaping first-launch scanning.
Keep all seeds, samples, coverage and outcome assertions. Use state barriers
for ownership and readiness; wait for completed metric samples before taking
an invariant snapshot. A fixture must not consume a product busy timeout just
to hold a process alive while starting unrelated contenders. Observe the actual
ownership sidecar lock while SQLite is held, then release SQLite and retain the
owner at its output boundary during competitor startup. An address-file hint
alone is not a lock assertion. Supply refusal-case
stdin before launching the child: a correct early exit must not race a parent's
later pipe write. Process diagnostics are separate named line records. A queued
host diagnostic can arrive after the direct lifecycle exit write; parse the
matching event line and retain its complete reason comparison, without treating
the rest of stderr as part of that record. Require the expected event exactly
once so unrelated output does not conceal duplicate or malformed events.
Browser fixtures remove request routes and wait for active callbacks before
closing their browser. A callback still owns live browser I/O; teardown must
join it, preserving callback errors instead of ignoring them.

Compiler-group cleanup uses two complete empty process snapshots. A timed-out
leaf `ps` invocation supplies no usable bytes; retry that timeout once within
the existing sixty-second total drain deadline. Other inspector errors and
malformed output refuse immediately. A retry never authorizes dispatch or
reuses partial output, and the two fresh empty snapshots remain mandatory.

**Immutable browser fixtures (PW, 2026-10-05).** A temporary Vite root has no
package manifest. Prebundle its known CommonJS React entry points before serving,
disable dependency discovery, HMR and file watching. HMR suppression alone does
not stop the optimizer invalidating already-served modules. Use the shared
`test/helpers/browser-server.mjs` for immutable fixtures, retaining each test's
aliases, plugins, routes and explicit reloads. Each root owns its optimizer
cache, including when TMPDIR sits below a package. Canonicalize the filesystem
allow list on Darwin. Bind the real fixture socket with port zero: Vite 8's
probe-close-bind startup can lose its selected port while initializing. Retain
Vite initialization and use its resolved URLs from the real listening socket;
these immutable fixtures do not restart or rely on its remembered probe port.
The per-row fixture records server, websocket, navigation,
crash and closure events and rejects optimizer reload requests even when HMR
would suppress them. Every converted fixture also guards main-frame navigation
from its initial load through teardown. Intentional reloads declare a single
navigation allowance scoped to that reload; violations remain failures even
if readiness succeeds in the replacement document. Reference-app history/hash
routes have an explicit same-document allowance, distinguished through Chromium
navigation events so it cannot excuse a document replacement. A native child-test
regression verifies the failure exit as well as the allowed reload/route cases.
The revocation-retention qualifier shares these fixtures and private caches.
A late-entry regression exercises the real optimizer; no whole-test retry or
weaker render assertion is permitted. See the
[PW report](../snapback4/reports/20261005-pw-per-row-navigation/REPORT.md), including
the distinction between controlled attribution and the uninstrumented 0.4.13
failure.

For the single-threaded in-memory Clique fixture, pair and rotate ordinary,
dense and celebrity measurements within each run. Keep the three-times
regression ceiling and all three warmups/twenty samples, but apply that ceiling
to thread CPU time. Record wall p95 alongside it for diagnosis; CPU contention
is not evidence of extra engine work. This is a fixture regression guard,
not a wall-latency benchmark or a publication performance claim.

Model hang guards are aggregate budgets scaled at two seconds per hosted
history: 10,000 seconds per session tier, 6,000 seconds for online interleavings
and 1,800 seconds per mixed-evidence host at default counts. They are not
individual two-second deadlines; a stuck history may consume the aggregate budget. The mixed-evidence
oracle runs three fresh hosted schedules per seed (mixed, generation and
notification), so its aggregate guard counts all three; an aborted test stops
starting new schedules. Seed generation, fair recovery, coverage and outcome
assertions remain unchanged. Browser expiry uses a controlled browser clock
and the dev server's clock control, with real React, HTTP and device storage. Startup and browser
IPC scheduling are outside the semantic expiry clock. Observe expiry timer
wakeups themselves: a polling callback can check a deadline and rearm without
evaluating a query. Independent server-clock
estimation remains covered by the skew tests. A callback can be delayed by a
busy or suspended host; the guide must not promise a wall-clock frame bound.

Readiness and other per-state guards detect a hang; they are not throughput
assertions. Timeout-mode tests still withhold the named progress event and
assert that the intended timeout and cleanup occur, rather than accidentally
failing during process startup. Never turn a product race into a pass by
increasing a guard. A separate fixture watchdog bounds a broken driver timeout
and cleans up its owned process group before failing the test. Preserve a reproducer and fix the product or file a ticket.

**2026-10-02 — QF qualification fixtures.** A printed dev URL precedes
initial named-worker admission; the worker fixture waits for a served HTTP
response before introducing a declaration whose Node probe is deliberately
broken. Timeout evidence retains the server exit state/log and worker statuses.
The replacement-browser fixture copies identity tables in one transaction,
retries SQLite contention for at most five seconds, and retains server custody
until every setup assertion succeeds; all setup failures close that server.

**2026-10-03 — QF Round 7 native execution and keeper-owned cleanup.** Each
file runs as native `node --test <file>` with default process isolation. A tiny
Node keeper is the first member of a fresh POSIX group and starts Node's test
parent in that group. It never exits on test completion; its referenced stdin
pipe holds it alive. No compiler or host-specific helper is needed. Ownership
is only that group. Ancestry links, sessions, spawn registries and environment
tokens confer no cleanup authority; the Round 1–4 escape policy remains superseded.

The runner **never sends a process-group signal**. On completion, stall or
abort it closes the keeper's stdin pipe. The keeper calls `kill(0, SIGKILL)`
on data, EOF or a pipe error, including EOF when the runner dies. That live
caller necessarily belongs to its own current group; no cached PGID or JS
exit field authorizes a signal. An unexpectedly dead keeper is diagnosed as
`TEST_FILE_KEEPER_EXIT` and fails the file. Closing an already broken pipe
cannot signal a reused process or group. This replaces Round 6's invalid
assumption that null ChildProcess exit fields prove unreaped custody.
The runner can SIGKILL its own asynchronously spawned `ps` child on timeout or
output overflow, using that live ChildProcess handle; it never signals a test
process or a test group.

After requesting cleanup the runner only inspects. Verification requires two
consecutive inspections 50 ms apart with no living group member, including
the keeper. Any observed leftover fails a completed file as `TEST_FILE_LEAK`,
even if it subsequently exits; a stalled file retains `TEST_FILE_HANG`. Pipe
release is asynchronous, so verification can observe a leftover before the
keeper kills it. Zombies are exited. Verification has a five-second retry
window measured with per-file monotonic time. Expiry requires completed evidence,
not a timer-only refusal: a completed post-deadline snapshot with a live group
refuses. Two consecutive empty snapshots pass even if the second completes after
the deadline (PS Round 2, below). Failed inspection never establishes a pass.

A process disappearing between enumeration and identity inspection counts as
exited. Failed per-process metadata reads recheck existence; present malformed
metadata refuses with the original error and exact metadata bytes as base64.
The first inspection error survives later cleanup errors. Process listing
failure, timeout or empty output remains fail-closed as
`TEST_PROCESS_INSPECTION`. BSD ps runs with `LC_ALL=C`.

**2026-10-03 — QF Round 8 process-table isolation.** Every row's PID, PPID
and PGID must parse as nonnegative pid_t decimals (zero includes kernel rows).
An unclassifiable prefix refuses the inspection, even when an apparent PID
has vanished: it cannot prove that a row is unrelated. First build the full
parent index, then select the file's group and the transitive descendants of
its members, including descendants of the keeper when its own row is absent.
Selection is independent of row order and does not stop at zombies. Only
these selected rows have their UID, start, state and command fields validated
for the inspection, except the narrow transient-snapshot recognizer below.
Other rows count as `unrelatedRows` diagnostics, with no metadata validation
or liveness probe; their printing cannot change the file verdict. This keeps
all potentially related metadata inspectable without making another user's
metadata a prerequisite for a passing file. A selected row with malformed
metadata still refuses while its PID exists; verified disappearance remains
exited. The first error and its exact row bytes are retained.

UID accepts both unsigned Linux uid_t values and Darwin's signed decimal
presentation (including nobody = -2). C-locale lstart is parsed by its five
fields and normalized for whitespace, not sliced at 24 columns. State flags
are accepted as part of one token; Z is exited, while all other states remain
conservatively living. Command names occupy the rest of the line, including
spaces and parentheses. Linux identity still uses /proc start ticks.
**Round 9:** every fresh Darwin listing used for a file, including identity
rereads, first reclassifies the file's group and visible descendants from that
fresh table. Only then does an identity reread select the requested PID. A PID
reused outside this scope counts as the original process having exited, whether
the replacement's ancillary metadata is valid or malformed. Related malformed
metadata and unclassifiable routing prefixes still fail closed. Standalone
identity queries without a file scope select their requested PID.
The complete table is still obtained on every enumeration, with a 16 MiB
limit and the bounded timeout recovery below. The ancestry index is linear in
table size.

**2026-10-04 — QF2 exiting routing snapshots.** Recognize Linux `X` with
PGID `4294967295` or `-1`, or PPID zero with an otherwise valid PGID. On
Darwin recognize PGID `-1` (also its unsigned presentation) with `Z`, `?`,
or the `E` exiting flag; Darwin's `X` flag means traced, not dead. Require a
positive in-range PID, nonnegative in-range PPID, well-formed ancillary fields
and platform-appropriate state flags for this recognition. It authorizes only
discarding the **entire snapshot** and retrying, never selecting or dropping a
row, declaring its PID dead, or inferring ownership. Take at most three complete
snapshots, 50 ms apart, with the existing limits on each ps call. A successful
fresh table alone supplies scope and metadata. Persistent anomalies, failed
fresh listings and all other unclassifiable routing still refuse; keep the first
error and its exact bytes. Changing anomalous PIDs cannot renew the retry budget.
No existence-probe result exempts a damaged prefix. Darwin `?`, `E` and `X`
with valid routing remain conservatively living; only `Z` is classified exited
from an accepted ps row. Linux `/proc` identity and keeper-only signaling are
unchanged. This chooses bounded fresh snapshots over trusting an exiting row's
apparent PID; the [QF2 report](../snapback4/reports/20261004-qf2-exiting-pgid/REPORT.md)
records the platform source audit and regressions.

Ancestry here expands **metadata inspection only**. It never authorizes a
signal or counts an escaped descendant as an in-group leak. Cleanup custody
remains the keeper's own process group. The regressions include synthetic
Darwin/Linux tables, 5,000-row listings, malformed related and unrelated rows,
and a real PATH ps shim that appends the exact dry-run-7 system row.

**2026-10-06 — PS large process tables.** Each complete listing first has a
two-second budget. Only `ETIMEDOUT` discards the partial output and retries
once with a 60-second budget and the same columns, C locale and 16 MiB limit.
A failed retry, nonzero exit, empty output or other command error still refuses
as `TEST_PROCESS_INSPECTION`; a timeout never proves an empty group. The
three-fresh-snapshot routing retry and its first-error preservation are
unchanged; each fresh snapshot has its own bounded command retry.

Keep `lstart` in the complete table. Splitting routing and start metadata into
separate listings would need a new argument for PID reuse and for the exit-state
recognizer's metadata validation. A small Linux formatting cost does not supply
that argument. The [PS report](../snapback4/reports/20261005-ps-inspection-timeout/REPORT.md)
records injected real timeouts and an 11,178-row Linux measurement; it does not
claim a new Darwin run.

**Round 1:** the file watchdog and cleanup's five-second retry window use plain
monotonic time. A shared inspection discount
let concurrent persistent groups consume each other's polling intervals without
advancing either deadline, also starving a hung file's watchdog. Remove that
discount entirely. The production stall floor is 900 seconds, well above the
bounded 60-second ps retry. Completed or stopped files cannot rearm their
watchdog from queued progress. Observed living non-keeper leftovers still fail
a completed file as a leak.

**Round 2:** enumerate with asynchronous `spawn`, including fresh Darwin identity
rereads; await the same 2/60-second command bounds and 50 ms routing retry waits.
Keep the full table, C locale, 16 MiB combined output bound, three-snapshot routing
limit and first-error preservation. Timeout or overflow kills only that command
child and closes its pipes; await close/reaping before returning its refusal.
Only timeout authorizes the one command retry, and partial output never supplies
an empty group. Other files' timers, progress IPC and keeper pipe EOF continue
while a listing is in flight. A stalled or aborted file releases its keeper
before awaiting diagnostic inspection.

Cleanup retains its per-file five-second monotonic deadline. When the polling
window expires, complete another inspection rather than refusing from elapsed
time or stale rows. A snapshot that completes after the deadline and still shows
owned live processes refuses as `TEST_FILE_LEAK`. An empty snapshot always needs
a second consecutive empty completed snapshot, at least 50 ms later; that pair
passes even if the confirming snapshot completes after the deadline. Thus late
success is explicit, and a slow first empty snapshot cannot invent a leak of
nobody. A failed or repeatedly timed-out listing remains
`TEST_PROCESS_INSPECTION`. Each call retains its finite command/routing budgets;
after expiry, at most two completed snapshots decide live refusal or empty
confirmation. Pre-release or otherwise observed non-keeper leftovers retain
the existing fail-closed file verdict described above.

**Darwin regression (PD, 2026-10-06):** stale-enumeration fixtures must leave
the per-PID identity reread fresh. Linux reads `/proc` for that check; Darwin
calls the injected inspector again with `pid` and the file's `group`. Replaying
the old table into both calls fabricates a live identity indefinitely and
also mixes identity reads into the cleanup-snapshot count. Count completed
enumerations separately, require exactly two empty confirmations without
reset, bound their identity reads, and retain the earlier `TEST_FILE_LEAK`
without an inspection/deadline error. Exercise the Darwin branch on Linux as
well. This corrects the fixture; neither production platform path changes.
The [PD report](../snapback4/reports/20261006-pd-darwin-stale-rows/REPORT.md)
retains the native Darwin reproduction and verification.

**Limitation:** processes that leave the file's group through setsid, setpgid
or detached spawns are out of scope. They are not cleaned up or used
to establish a leak. Visible descendants still receive the metadata validation
described above; they are not tracked across snapshots or reparenting. Fixtures
that create such processes must manage their own lifetimes. This runner is
not an arbitrary-daemon sandbox or adopted subreaper.

Native Node's test-parent exit status is the sole test verdict. The reporter
is attached only with `--test-reporter`: it observes progress and summary
counts for diagnostics, never executes files or decides success. There is no
`run()` wrapper or `isolation: 'none'` path. Default `only`, TODO, skip, hook,
cancellation and late asynchronous-error semantics are Node's, including
in-file exit-code overrides. A passing file additionally requires successful
watchdog and group cleanup checks; those refusals remain independently named.

The file watchdog measures **lack of subtest progress**, not total duration.
The reporter observes actual `test:dequeue` (execution start) and
`test:complete` events from Node's native test parent and relays them over IPC.
Each delivered event resets the independent runner's timer. TAP formatting,
presentation-ordered `test:start` and console output are never the clock source.
Native isolation can buffer child events across synchronous subtests that do
not yield. Those events cannot renew the timer until delivered; that unreported
interval is subject to the same stall bound. The regression records this limit
with a shortened test-only bound rather than changing Node's execution model.
Asynchronous and nested tests continue to renew progress. The bound is
`max(900 seconds, largest single declared guard + max(60 seconds, 25% of guard))`,
including model seed overrides, with the same rule in serial mode. There is
**no absolute duration ceiling**: a file making real subtest progress may run
as long as its work requires. The watchdog's failure names the file, elapsed
time, idle interval, last subtest event, recent output and owned processes.

The round-2 census records every source-declared timeout and helper guard in
all 286 client/fixture files and checks each maximum plus margin against its
stall bound. Social-browser's largest guard is 726 seconds (907.5-second stall
bound); retained-history's 68 sequential 372-second guards use a 900-second
stall bound, reset for each start/finish, with no 900-second total limit.
Search, mixed, session and online default to stall bounds of respectively
10,740, 2,250, 12,500 and 7,500 seconds. The admission review's enclosing
6,832-second guard gives 8,540 seconds. Keep named work counts aligned with
their declarations and rerun the census when they change. The
[QF Round 2 report](../snapback4/reports/20261002-qf-qualification-flakes/REPORT.md#round-2--whole-group-cleanup-and-progress-watchdog-2026-10-03)
records every maximum/bound, actual stalls, the fork-during-snapshot regression,
full-count stress and complete suite outcomes.

Lane SL's [contention report](../snapback4/reports/20260929-sl-suite-under-load/REPORT.md)
records the original failures, corrective controls and complete suite runs on
CPUs 80–95 at publication concurrency, including 16/48 busy workers, delayed
child executable startup and the final unconfined run. Linux qualification
does not claim execution of Darwin-only witness-driver cases.

**2026-10-07 — FL fault dispatch checks continuing identity.** A complete
checkpoint or role record proves readiness, not continued lifetime: debug
checkpoints expire after twenty seconds. Test fault dispatch uses one shared
command/birth check immediately before signalling. Linux retains boot ID and
start ticks; Darwin retains the native microsecond birth through a test-only
`proc_pidinfo`/`KERN_PROCARGS2` inspector, rather than rounding to `ps lstart`.
Direct children capture birth and parent at spawn; null JS exit fields alone
never authorize dispatch. Missing, changed or uninspectable identities refuse
the fault, including a checkpoint delayed twenty-two seconds. The existence
probe with signal number zero is read-only and confers no ownership.

Private fixture requests and keeper EOF still make living actors signal
themselves/their own groups. Those paths never turn disk records into signal
targets. Immediate birth checks on the remaining numeric fault paths do not
claim a kernel handle or eliminate every exit-to-syscall race. The
[FL round-4 report](../snapback4/reports/20261007-fl-dry-run-flakes/round-4/REPORT.md)
records the complete signal-site audit and intercepted negative controls.

**2026-10-07 — FL owned-command timeouts remain reapable.** Fault dispatch
from recorded PIDs still requires current command and birth. Direct-child
capture also retains the kernel's complete interpreter argv, including the
script path. Timeout/overflow cleanup uses the retained native ChildProcess
handle immediately and waits for close; it never converts that handle into an
unchecked numeric PID. It retains the original timeout/overflow. As corrected
in FL round 9, cleanup does not wait for initial capture or attempt another
identity inspection: either can outlive the command's watchdog. Fault
injection still requires fresh identity. Untracked objects and disk records
cannot use this cleanup path.

The Darwin inspector is compiled asynchronously before its identity API is
used: thirty seconds to finish, SIGTERM, one second of grace, then SIGKILL
and reap. The leaf probe has a ten-second timeout and uses its owned handle
for SIGKILL and reaping. FL round 9 makes that probe asynchronous too; pending
initial capture cancels its probe when the target child exits. Neither a
blocking probe nor a SIGTERM-only timeout may hold up sibling watchdogs.
Keeper EOF and fixture self-signals use a synchronous helper independent of
inspector compilation. [FL round 5](../snapback4/reports/20261007-fl-dry-run-flakes/round-5/REPORT.md)
records the hang regressions and their independent cleanup controls.

**2026-10-07 — FL compiler custody includes subprocesses.** Compilation now
runs beneath a live Node keeper in a private process group. The caller closes
its owned pipe to request TERM, then the keeper sends KILL to its own group
after the grace period, including when the compiler driver already exited.
Normal completion also releases the group. Caller death closes the pipe and
starts the same cleanup. The caller does not send to a cached PGID. Before
reporting completed cleanup it reaps the keeper and requires two complete
observations, 50 ms apart, with no live group member. Zombies are exited;
failed inspection is an explicit cleanup error. The bootstrap inspector uses
the system ps directly, without recursively compiling an identity inspector.
Processes deliberately leaving this group remain outside its custody.

Tracked direct-child dispatch now checks identity and then uses its retained
native handle. Recorded-PID dispatch still rechecks command and exact birth
immediately before the signal, but those userspace checks are not atomic with
the syscall: exit and PID reuse in that small interval can redirect a signal.
This is a remaining race, not an absolute guarantee against signalling an
unowned PID. Prefer group or native-handle custody for processes the test owns.
[FL round 6](../snapback4/reports/20261007-fl-dry-run-flakes/round-6/REPORT.md)
records the real GCC reproduction and subprocess regressions.

**2026-10-08 — FL nested correctness work is not a launch benchmark.** The
real runner CLI parity fixture, native Node baselines and export command
fixtures use the ordinary fifteen-minute file-stall floor as their command
work allowance. Multiple sequential invocations have an aggregate test guard
plus two minutes for cleanup. Their larger declared guards are reflected in
`largestGuard`, including its existing scheduling margin; the outer file
watchdog must not silently restore the old short limit. The export-background
cases share one work-plus-cleanup guard while keeping each existing state
observation and product-deadline assertion.

Nested CLI and ordinary native Node baselines await owned command completion
asynchronously. Timeout reports `TEST_FIXTURE_COMMAND_TIMEOUT` distinctly and
releases the command's private group; detached file keepers also receive EOF
when the runner exits. Native verdicts, counts, failure text, shim execution
and inspection/keeper-error refusals remain assertions. Deliberately exercised
watchdog and compiler-deadline clocks remain distinct from these correctness
work allowances. A real hung-runner control must still fail and drain its
descendants; delayed-startup controls must preserve all original verdicts.
Initial Linux child capture can observe an empty cmdline during an interpreter
exec. It may wait up to two minutes for complete bytes, anchored to the first
live birth and the spawning parent. Changed births, malformed nonempty commands
and other read failures refuse. **FL round 9:** this wait yields through
abortable ten-millisecond timers. Spawn returns the owned child immediately;
the command watchdog is armed before capture and cleanup never awaits it.
Child exit/error cancels pending capture, including a native probe. Signal
APIs are awaitable: callers must await initial capture and the fresh exact
command/birth checks before dispatch. Identity checks themselves do not retry
incomplete observations. No blocking wait or synchronous subprocess runs in
the shared runner, signal or command helpers. Recorded-PID inspection is still
not atomic with the syscall; owned handles/groups remain preferred. The
[round-9 report](../snapback4/reports/20261007-fl-dry-run-flakes/round-9/REPORT.md)
records heartbeat, timeout/reaping and anchor mutation controls.
Load verification must also keep its executable inode stable: rebuilding a
CLI whose live workers re-exec `current_exe()` invalidates that concurrent run.
See [FL round 8](../snapback4/reports/20261007-fl-dry-run-flakes/round-8/REPORT.md).
