15-  return insert profiles { owner: viewer, name }
16-```
17-
18-Reference contexts project required rows’ `.id`. Media: decimal KB/MB/GB.
19-
20-Columns/arguments become nullable with `?`; omission becomes null. Table types are references; neither typing nor membership reads the target: use `require exists t[id] else CODE` or `r = t[id] ! else CODE`.
21:Defaults fill omissions; explicit values win. Formats: handle/url/email. `format email` is the shared ASCII address contract described below; identity providers may apply additional policy.
22:Money/decimal are scaled integers; fractions refuse E_INPUT. Divide them by an int with an explicit rounding mode, for example `divide(amount, 3, 'half-even')`. To produce a fractional decimal from integer inputs, name its result scale: `divide(total, count, 2, 'half-even')` returns `decimal 2`; no floating point or manual `total * 100` is introduced. Identical admitted unique-key reads narrow after exists/non-null guards, including pure helper arguments; writes, rebinding and viewer changes invalidate proof. A matching `require count(domain) > 0` also makes `min`, `max` and `sum` of a required column non-null until a write or authority change invalidates that proof.
23-
24-## Limits
25-
26-- Guard or assert nullable values before passing them to required parameters: E_QUARRY_TYPE.
27-- Values and arithmetic must fit the integer range: E_OVERFLOW.
28-- `/` and `%` take ints and floor: `-7 / 2` is -4, `-7 % 2` is 1. A divisor must be nonzero: E_DIV_ZERO.
29:- `divide(value, int, mode)` preserves int/decimal/money and requires one of `toward-zero`, `away-from-zero`, `floor`, `ceiling`, `half-away-from-zero` or `half-even` as a literal mode. It uses checked integer quotient/remainder arithmetic; zero refuses E_DIV_ZERO and overflow refuses E_OVERFLOW.
30:- `divide(int, int, scale, mode)` returns `decimal scale`; `scale` is a literal from 0 through 12. Scaling uses a checked wide integer, then applies the same signed rounding rule and i53 result check. Null inputs are never zero.
31-- New required defaults must also fit the existing store: E_ADOPT.
32-
33-## Details
34-
35-Types describe the values a column or argument accepts.
36-They also set limits on text, lists and uploaded assets.
37-
38-### Column types
39-
40-```quarry
41-table everything:
42-  who: principal                      -- the authenticated subject; `viewer` is one
43-  tag: id                             -- an opaque identity
44-  parent: everything ?                -- a reference to a table's row: its id
45-  n: int                              -- an integer within ±(2^53 − 1); there are no floats
46-  at: time                            -- milliseconds since the epoch; `now` is one
--
98-### Text bounds
99-
100-`text` defaults to `text <=10000` characters in columns, arguments and helper
101-parameters. Write `text <=N` or `text M..N` when the bound is a product rule;
102-an indexed column must also fit the key budget (`guide indexes`).
103-
104:`format email` accepts one ASCII `@`, a 1–64 character local part made from
105-letters, digits and `!#$%&'*+-/=?^_`{|}~` atoms separated by single dots, and a
106-1–253 character domain made from 1–63 character ASCII letter/digit/hyphen
107-labels. Dots cannot lead, trail or repeat, and a domain label cannot begin or
108-end with `-`. A single domain label such as `alice@localhost` is allowed.
109-The engine does not normalize case or Unicode; store a separate normalized
110-lookup column when the product needs case-insensitive identity matching.
111-
112-## Engine-assigned per-parent sequences
113-
114-`ordinal: sequence by conversationId` is an `int` assigned by the engine
115-inside the write transaction. The named column must be a required reference
116-on this table, such as `conversationId: conversations`. E_SEQUENCE_PARENT
117-names a missing, optional or non-reference parent column and gives the rewrite.
118-The ordinal and parent reference are immutable. Do not
119-supply a value or default. A table named `sequence` remains a reference type;
120-only the two words `sequence by` introduce an ordinal.

//! Search card, compiled with every other keyword card by tests/guide.rs.
pub const CARD: &str = concat!(r##"# Search: word prefixes, on the server and on the device

**Start here:** Declare `search` on text or enum columns, then use `matching`, order and a cursor; the planner builds the word index. This directory query uses the profiles schema below; reset the cursor when the text changes and show incomplete results honestly.

```quarry
query people(q: text <=100, c: cursor ?):
  return profiles matching q after c first 20 by .handle
```

- `E_QUARRY_TYPE`: a search column is neither text nor enum; use one of those types before listing it in `search`.
- `N_SEARCH_UNSCOPED`: authorization varies across the scan; pin the intended room, or preserve the wider domain and show partial results.
- `E_CURSOR_INDEX`: an explicit index lacks a total order; use `by .column` so the planner supplies the page index.

## Reference

### Search one conversation, newest first, paged

Use the conversation's unique index order (or equivalently `by .ordinal`):

```quarry
table conversations:
  read <- exists members[.id, viewer]
  sync to members[.id]
table members:
  conversation: conversations
  principal: principal
  unique conversation, principal
  cap 50 by conversation
  read <- exists members[.conversation, viewer]
  sync to members[.conversation]
table messages:
  conversation: conversations
  ordinal: sequence by conversation
  body: text <=2000
  search body
  unique byConversationOrdinal: conversation, ordinal
  read <- exists members[.conversation, viewer]
  sync to members[.conversation] last 500 by byConversationOrdinal
query searchChat(conversation: conversations, q: text <=100, c: cursor ?):
  return messages[conversation = conversation] matching q last 20 by byConversationOrdinal after c
```

Both forms plan `(conversation, words(body), ordinal, id)`; the unique index still serves history and receipts.
A named plain unique index supplies its order; named bracket keys must sit inside its leading prefix. Omitting keys orders by the whole index. Other plain indexes use column order.
Existing integer ordinals work too: do not migrate to `sequence by` just to search (`guide sequences`).
Pass `next` as `c`; reset it when the conversation or text changes. An empty needle browses in ordinal order.
Keep ordinals stable while paging; refresh to include new matches before the cursor. Point decorations preserve the bare page (below).
When server and offline device searches scan the same set of messages, their ordering and examined-row,
index-entry, step and output-byte counts match. A device searches acquired history: show
`complete: false` honestly, even for an empty result beyond its horizon; follow `next` only when present.

Plain `check` counts and summarizes search warnings: unscoped completeness (`N_SEARCH_UNSCOPED`), word expansion (`N_SEARCH_WORDS`), opening refusal (`N_SEARCH_OPEN`), and per-hit roster work (`N_SEARCH_SHAPE`). `--verbose` adds explanations and rewrites; `--cost` adds execution costs.

An empty needle, including whitespace or punctuation that folds to no terms, browses in the query's order under the same read rule. This directory uses one query, ordered by handle, with `next` to continue; reset the cursor when changing the search text. For a screen that wants nothing until typing, put `if q = '': return []` before the read.

`search body, caption` declares the searchable columns. Enum columns use the
same spelling, for example `search category`; their stored variant text is
tokenized exactly like text (`'coffee-shop'` contributes `coffee` and `shop`).
The planner supplies the words index: one entry per distinct word of the named text or enum columns, in that position, folded the same way everywhere
(NFC, lowercase, a word boundary at every character that is not a letter
or digit). The columns before it are the search's scope; the columns
after it order the page and end in `id`, so a cursor continues it. A
words index serves `matching` and nothing else, and cannot be unique.

Indexed tokens and query terms are both truncated to 64 Unicode characters
after folding (at most 256 UTF-8 bytes). Longer terms therefore search with
that truncated prefix. The full composite word entry, including scope,
ordering columns and appended id, must fit the portable 2048-byte index key.

`matching q` folds the text the same way. Its first term selects every
word it is a prefix of ("cof": coffee, coffers, cofounder — one seek each,
bounded by the meter for a uniform scope; at most 32 words for an admitted
non-uniform scope, whose page remains `complete: false` even below that bound), and those words
merge as ordered lanes into one page — newest first with `last 20 by .at`. Every further term must be a prefix of some word of the row ("cof
car" finds "COFFEE for Carol"). Mid-word text does not match; an expired row is gone at once;
an edit re-tokenizes. A uniform scope (the rule decided by the room)
costs one verdict per scope; with a total unique `(chatKey, principal)` key
on t, `.principal = viewer or exists t[.chatKey, viewer]` is decided by the
chat alone because the row is its own witness, so a roster page pinned by
the chat is complete and no second table is needed. An unscoped search under a per-room rule
checks the rule per row and is never `complete` (`N_NEVER_COMPLETE` names
it), except for the inferred ordinary server audience described below;
use the grouped or newest form below, or read on the device.

The newest form below merges the viewer's rooms by `at` and `id`, with
one verdict per scope when its prefix decides the rule. Its uniform lane
source runs under the examined meter: `check --cost` prints `lanes ≤ meter`;
an explicit `first N` above 50 still emits N_WIDE_MERGE. Use the grouped form,
which pages memberships and includes each chat's hits, when opening all rooms
cannot fit.
A pinned conjunct such as `exists members[.roomId, viewer]` can deny a scope
before word discovery even when another conjunct needs each row. Admitted
non-uniform scopes keep per-row costs and remain incomplete; hidden rows
there still spend the query-wide examined/index-entry budget. The admitted non-uniform scope's 32-word
bound records a word cut without cutting later lanes; operator
results name it in `word_cut_at`, separately from a meter cut in `capped_at`.
Device reads and uniform server reads expose `cut: 'words'` or `cut: 'meter'`
beside `complete`; `meter` wins if both limits cut. Non-uniform server reads
report `cut: null`: their reason is withheld, with `complete: false` still truthful.
`null` means "not disclosed here", not "not cut". Render "Results may be incomplete"
for `complete: false` with `cut: null`, even when empty; never "no matches".
The precise reason remains in development-only `Read.meter.cut`; `serve` never
returns that meter. If the server cannot answer the query, declare `query … held:`
to answer from the device; the server refuses it with E_QUERY_HELD. Call a held
query through a local client over acquired partitions; it has no server fallback. Use the grouped
membership read for a server answer.
Uniform word expansion is bounded by the meter. A cut during discovery or
opening returns `complete: false, cut: 'meter'` with no continuation.
Heap exhaustion clears the page: `data: null`, `complete: false`,
`cut: 'meter'`, no `next`.
Merged pages return no `next` for a non-uniform answer, an opening cut or a
word-limit cut. A resumable read-meter cut while an owning uniform lazy merge drains established heads
can carry `next` after its last fully shaped emitted row (§5.6), subject to final-answer eligibility, with `complete: false, cut: 'meter'`.
Heap exhaustion clears the whole page even during the drain; step exhaustion aborts and output exhaustion can refuse after materialisation.

 succeeded in 0ms:
//! Bind key operands once, retaining authored sequencing and contextual literals.
use super::*;

impl<'a> Binder<'a> {
    pub(super) fn bind_key_parts(&mut self, info: &TableInfo, read: &ReadExpr, end: usize,
        keys: &mut Vec<(Option<String>, Expr, Ty)>, out: &mut setup::Body) -> Fallible<()> {
        for (position, part) in read.keys.iter().enumerate().take(end).skip(keys.len()) {
            let mut setup = setup::Body::default();
            let expected = part.column.as_deref().or_else(|| read.index.as_ref().and_then(|name| info.indexes.iter().find(|i| &i.name == name)).and_then(|i| i.columns.get(position)).map(String::as_str)).and_then(|column| key_identity(info, column));
            let (e, t) = match expected {
                Some(expected) => self.expr_as_reference(&part.value, &mut setup, &expected)?,
                None => self.expr(&part.value, &mut setup)?,
            };
            self.sequence_setup(keys.iter_mut().map(|(_, e, _)| e), setup, out);
            let e = if expr::allocates(&e) { self.snapshot(e, out) } else { e };
            keys.push((part.column.clone(), e, t));
        }
        if let Some(index) = read.index.as_ref().and_then(|name| info.indexes.iter().find(|i| &i.name == name)) {
            for (position,(column,value,ty)) in keys.iter_mut().enumerate() {
                if let Some(expected) = column.as_deref().or_else(|| index.columns.get(position).map(String::as_str)).and_then(|c| key_identity(info,c)) {
                    (*value,*ty) = self.row_identity(value.clone(),ty.clone(),&expected);
                }
            }
        }
        Ok(())
    }

    // Mixed-key normalization names an already-bound positional prefix. Only
    // a literal acquires new context; compound expressions keep their IR/setup.
    pub(super) fn name_bound_keys(&mut self, info: &TableInfo, read: &ReadExpr,
        keys: &mut [(Option<String>, Expr, Ty)]) -> Fallible<()> {
        for ((column, expr, ty), part) in keys.iter_mut().zip(&read.keys) {
            *column = part.column.clone();
            if let Some(expected) = column.as_deref().and_then(|column| key_identity(info, column))
                && matches!(&part.value, A::Literal { value: Literal::Str(_), .. }) {
                (*expr, *ty) = self.expr_as_reference(&part.value, &mut setup::Body::default(), &expected)?;
            }
        }
        Ok(())
    }
}

fn key_identity(info: &TableInfo, column: &str) -> Option<Ty> {
    let ty = if column == "id" { Ty::Ref(info.decl.name.clone()) }
        else { Ty::from_scalar(info.columns.get(column)?) };
    ty.required().is_identity().then_some(ty)
}

NOT READY

1. **[P1] Bare global totals still take the page path.** At `0fe7cc1263:snapback4/crates/snapback4-lang/src/bind/program/expr.rs:129`, the generated read has no keys. `prefix.rs:10` classifies every empty-key read as a page, and `program.rs:375` returns the scan before reaching the synthetic-key insertion or scalar extraction. Consequently, `return total` returns a page rather than the required number/null; populated results can expose `$global`. The reported global-total test evidence does not establish this committed revision’s behavior.

2. **[P1] A fresh capture can revive stale count provenance.** Capture markers contain only the domain identity (`0fe7cc1263:snapback4/crates/snapback4-lang/src/bind/program/aggregate_facts.rs:93`), and subsequent captures reinstall that same marker on domain roots (`:105`). For example:

   ```quarry
   who = owner
   n = count(entries[who] by byOwner)
   who = next
   m = count(entries[who] by byOwner)
   require n > 0 else EMPTY
   return min(entries[who] by byOwner, .score) + 1
   ```

   Rebinding preserves `who`’s runtime identity. Capturing `m` restores its marker, while `n` retains the original matching marker; the checks at `:123` therefore accept stale `n`. If `owner` has rows and `next` has none, the binder incorrectly treats the empty domain’s minimum as required. Captures need definition/version identity.

3. **[P2] The specified email write refusal code differs from implementation.** `38bef8f6d8:llp/3000.013.001-wave-12-semantic-language-gaps.spec.md:39` promises `E_INPUT` at argument and write boundaries. However, `snapback4/crates/snapback4-core/src/engine/validate.rs:70` converts column-format failures into `RuntimeError::Constraint`, producing `E_CONSTRAINT`. Passing malformed email through an ordinary text argument and inserting it into an email-formatted column demonstrates the mismatch; the added email tests exercise formatted arguments instead.

The concatenated commit digest matches the supplied SHA-256. Findings are from committed code paths; tests were not rerun.
